I guess I haven’t really paid attention to whether it’s guilt exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s actually the part that scares me. It’s like the stopping part already slipped away from me. And then I’ll get distracted by work again for a second, like checking a file export or whatever, and somehow I’m eating more before I’ve really chosen to.\n\nDoctor: How often do these late-night eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Um… I didn’t realize it was that regular until I started kind of checking my delivery receipts, because I kept telling myself it was just “busy weeks.” But when I look at those and my calendar, there’s usually at least one night a week where it happens.\n\nSometimes it’s more than that, especially if a deadline overlaps with, like, client revisions or I’m staying up too late messing with layouts. I… I don’t really know. I don’t think I’ve been tracking that in a clear way.

Like, sometimes the next day is weird because if I’m rushing to get a project out, I might not eat at a normal time anyway, but that happens even when there wasn’t a late-night thing. It’s more chaotic than that, I guess. I haven’t really noticed myself making a plan like, “Okay, don’t eat today because of last night.”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your eating habits are causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m not sure how to separate the eating part from the general stress, if that makes sense. I mean, I know I don’t feel great afterward, but “guilty” feels like a specific word, you know?\n\nSometimes I’m more just sitting there thinking, why did I do that, especially if there are wrappers or containers around. But then my brain jumps to, like, the deadline, or whether the client is going to hate the mockup, or if I remembered to send the invoice—sorry. I guess I haven’t really paid attention to whether it’s guilt exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s actually the part that scares me. Like, once I start, there’s this point where I’m sort of watching myself keep going back—clicking back to the food app, or opening another package in the kitchen—and I’m thinking, okay, stop now, that’s enough.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like a normal decision anymore? It’s like the stopping part already slipped away from me. And then I’ll get distracted by work again for a second, like checking a file export or whatever, and somehow I’m eating more before I’ve really chosen to.\n\nDoctor: How often do these late-night eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Um… I didn’t realize it was that regular until I started kind of checking my delivery receipts, because I kept telling myself it was just “busy weeks.” But when I look at those and my calendar, there’s usually at least one night a week where it happens.\n\nSometimes it’s more than that, especially if a deadline overlaps with, like, client revisions or I’m staying up too late messing with layouts. But… yeah, it’s been that kind of weekly pattern for several months now, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these late-night eating episodes by doing something to counteract the calories, like exercising more or skipping meals the next day?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know. I don’t think I’ve been tracking that in a clear way.\n\nLike, sometimes the next day is weird because if I’m rushing to get a project out, I might not eat at a normal time anyway, but that happens even when there wasn’t a late-night thing. And exercise—honestly, I’ll get these ideas like, “I should go for a walk,” but then I end up sitting at my desk adjusting kerning for two hours and forget what time it is.\n\nSo… I’m not sure if I’m doing it to make up for calories, exactly. I haven’t really noticed a pattern like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for the calories from these late-night eating episodes by restricting your food intake the next day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, the next day I might not eat much until later, but that also happens on normal workdays when I get sucked into a project and suddenly it’s 3 p.m. and I’ve only had coffee. \n\nSo I don’t know if it’s like… me deliberately trying to “make up” for it. It’s more chaotic than that, I guess. I haven’t really noticed myself making a plan like, “Okay, don’t eat today because of last night.”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your eating habits are causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m not sure how to separate the eating part from the general stress, if that makes sense. Like, I’m stressed a lot because of work, and then the food stuff kind of gets mixed into that, and then I’m annoyed at myself for spending money on delivery when I already have groceries, which reminds me I still haven’t cleaned out my fridge from last week—\n\nSorry. Um. I guess it does stress me out some, yeah, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s the eating habits by themselves or everything around it. It’s hard for me to tell.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know. I don’t weigh myself consistently, so I don’t have, like, a chart in my head or anything.\n\nMy clothes feel different sometimes, but then I also have clothes from three different “eras” of my life, if that makes sense—like old work pants from when I actually went into an office, and then stretchy at-home clothes, and then random thrifted stuff that never fit right in the first place. So it’s hard to tell from that.\n\nI haven’t noticed anything that feels really dramatic, I guess. But I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Like, not as a thing I do after. I’ve felt nauseous sometimes if I eat too fast or too much, but I don’t—sorry, I’m trying to think if there was ever a time where I did it on purpose.\n\nI don’t really like throwing up at all. I’ll do that thing where I lie really still and scroll on my phone and tell myself I’m fine, which is probably ridiculous because then I end up watching random videos until like 2 a.m.—but no, I don’t think I make myself vomit after.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I mean, the next day I might not eat much until later, but that also happens on normal workdays when I get sucked into a project and suddenly it’s 3 p.m. and I’ve only had coffee. \n\nSo I don’t know if it’s like… me deliberately trying to “make up” for it. It’s more chaotic than that, I guess. I haven’t really noticed myself making a plan like, “Okay, don’t eat today because of last night.”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your eating habits are causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m not sure how to separate the eating part from the general stress, if that makes sense. Like, I’m stressed a lot because of work, and then the food stuff kind of gets mixed into that, and then I’m annoyed at myself for spending money on delivery when I already have groceries, which reminds me I still haven’t cleaned out my fridge from last week—\n\nSorry. Um. I guess it does stress me out some, yeah, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s the eating habits by themselves or everything around it. It’s hard for me to tell.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know. I don’t weigh myself consistently, so I don’t have, like, a chart in my head or anything.\n\nMy clothes feel different sometimes, but then I also have clothes from three different “eras” of my life, if that makes sense—like old work pants from when I actually went into an office, and then stretchy at-home clothes, and then random thrifted stuff that never fit right in the first place. So it’s hard to tell from that.\n\nI haven’t noticed anything that feels really dramatic, I guess. But I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Like, not as a thing I do after. I’ve felt nauseous sometimes if I eat too fast or too much, but I don’t—sorry, I’m trying to think if there was ever a time where I did it on purpose.\n\nI don’t really like throwing up at all. I’ll do that thing where I lie really still and scroll on my phone and tell myself I’m fine, which is probably ridiculous because then I end up watching random videos until like 2 a.m.—but no, I don’t think I make myself vomit after.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there are times when you eat a large amount of food in a short period, like during those late-night episodes, without doing anything to try to counteract the calories afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s basically what happens. Like, on late work nights especially, I’ll order what I’m calling dinner, but then it’s not just dinner. It’ll be an entrée, and then sides, and dessert, and then somehow I’m also in the kitchen getting chips or whatever snack is there.\n\nAnd it’s in this weird short window where I’m still kind of at my desk, half-working, half-watching something, and then I look at the containers and it’s like… that was a lot. Definitely more than I’d eat if someone was sitting across from me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah… not really. I mean, at the very beginning I tell myself it’s just dinner, or just one snack, and I’m making a choice, you know? Um. I guess it does stress me out some, yeah, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s the eating habits by themselves or everything around it. It’s hard for me to tell.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know. Like, on late work nights especially, I’ll order what I’m calling dinner, but then it’s not just dinner. It’ll be an entrée, and then sides, and dessert, and then somehow I’m also in the kitchen getting chips or whatever snack is there.\n\nAnd it’s in this weird short window where I’m still kind of at my desk, half-working, half-watching something, and then I look at the containers and it’s like… that was a lot. It’s more chaotic than that, I guess. I haven’t really noticed myself making a plan like, “Okay, don’t eat today because of last night.”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your eating habits are causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m not sure how to separate the eating part from the general stress, if that makes sense. Like, I’m stressed a lot because of work, and then the food stuff kind of gets mixed into that, and then I’m annoyed at myself for spending money on delivery when I already have groceries, which reminds me I still haven’t cleaned out my fridge from last week—\n\nSorry. Um. I guess it does stress me out some, yeah, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s the eating habits by themselves or everything around it. It’s hard for me to tell.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know. I don’t weigh myself consistently, so I don’t have, like, a chart in my head or anything.\n\nMy clothes feel different sometimes, but then I also have clothes from three different “eras” of my life, if that makes sense—like old work pants from when I actually went into an office, and then stretchy at-home clothes, and then random thrifted stuff that never fit right in the first place. So it’s hard to tell from that.\n\nI haven’t noticed anything that feels really dramatic, I guess. But I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Like, not as a thing I do after. I’ll do that thing where I lie really still and scroll on my phone and tell myself I’m fine, which is probably ridiculous because then I end up watching random videos until like 2 a.m.—but no, I don’t think I make myself vomit after.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there are times when you eat a large amount of food in a short period, like during those late-night episodes, without doing anything to try to counteract the calories afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s basically what happens. Like, on late work nights especially, I’ll order what I’m calling dinner, but then it’s not just dinner. It’ll be an entrée, and then sides, and dessert, and then somehow I’m also in the kitchen getting chips or whatever snack is there.\n\nAnd it’s in this weird short window where I’m still kind of at my desk, half-working, half-watching something, and then I look at the containers and it’s like… that was a lot. Definitely more than I’d eat if someone was sitting across from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not really. I mean, at the very beginning I tell myself it’s just dinner, or just one snack, and I’m making a choice, you know? But then there’s this point where I’m kind of watching myself keep going back to the app or the kitchen, like clicking back to the food, opening another package, and I’m thinking, “Okay, stop, that’s enough.”\n\nAnd then I just… don’t. It feels like the decision already moved somewhere else, if that makes sense. I’m aware of it, but I don’t feel like I’m steering anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes, looking back on them the next day or at any other time?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. And then I’ll get distracted by work again for a second, like checking a file export or whatever, and somehow I’m eating more before I’ve really chosen to.\n\nDoctor: How often do these late-night eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Um… I didn’t realize it was that regular until I started kind of checking my delivery receipts, because I kept telling myself it was just “busy weeks.” But when I look at those and my calendar, there’s usually at least one night a week where it happens.\n\nSometimes it’s more than that, especially if a deadline overlaps with, like, client revisions or I’m staying up too late messing with layouts. But… yeah, it’s been that kind of weekly pattern for several months now, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these late-night eating episodes by doing something to counteract the calories, like exercising more or skipping meals the next day?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know. I don’t think I’ve been tracking that in a clear way.\n\nLike, sometimes the next day is weird because if I’m rushing to get a project out, I might not eat at a normal time anyway, but that happens even when there wasn’t a late-night thing. And exercise—honestly, I’ll get these ideas like, “I should go for a walk,” but then I end up sitting at my desk adjusting kerning for two hours and forget what time it is.\n\nSo… I’m not sure if I’m doing it to make up for calories, exactly. I haven’t really noticed a pattern like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for the calories from these late-night eating episodes by restricting your food intake the next day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, the next day I might not eat much until later, but that also happens on normal workdays when I get sucked into a project and suddenly it’s 3 p.m. and I’ve only had coffee. \n\nSo I don’t know if it’s like… me deliberately trying to “make up” for it. It’s more chaotic than that, I guess. I haven’t really noticed myself making a plan like, “Okay, don’t eat today because of last night.”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your eating habits are causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m not sure how to separate the eating part from the general stress, if that makes sense. Like, I’m stressed a lot because of work, and then the food stuff kind of gets mixed into that, and then I’m annoyed at myself for spending money on delivery when I already have groceries, which reminds me I still haven’t cleaned out my fridge from last week—\n\nSorry. Um. I guess it does stress me out some, yeah, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s the eating habits by themselves or everything around it. It’s hard for me to tell.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know. I don’t weigh myself consistently, so I don’t have, like, a chart in my head or anything.\n\nMy clothes feel different sometimes, but then I also have clothes from three different “eras” of my life, if that makes sense—like old work pants from when I actually went into an office, and then stretchy at-home clothes, and then random thrifted stuff that never fit right in the first place. So it’s hard to tell from that.\n\nI haven’t noticed anything that feels really dramatic, I guess. But I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Like, not as a thing I do after. I’ve felt nauseous sometimes if I eat too fast or too much, but I don’t—sorry, I’m trying to think if there was ever a time where I did it on purpose.\n\nI don’t really like throwing up at all. I’ll do that thing where I lie really still and scroll on my phone and tell myself I’m fine, which is probably ridiculous because then I end up watching random videos until like 2 a.m.—but no, I don’t think I make myself vomit after.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there are times when you eat a large amount of food in a short period, like during those late-night episodes, without doing anything to try to counteract the calories afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s basically what happens. Like, on late work nights especially, I’ll order what I’m calling dinner, but then it’s not just dinner. It’ll be an entrée, and then sides, and dessert, and then somehow I’m also in the kitchen getting chips or whatever snack is there.\n\nAnd it’s in this weird short window where I’m still kind of at my desk, half-working, half-watching something, and then I look at the containers and it’s like… that was a lot. Definitely more than I’d eat if someone was sitting across from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not really. I mean, at the very beginning I tell myself it’s just dinner, or just one snack, and I’m making a choice, you know? But then there’s this point where I’m kind of watching myself keep going back to the app or the kitchen, like clicking back to the food, opening another package, and I’m thinking, “Okay, stop, that’s enough.”\n\nAnd then I just… don’t. It feels like the decision already moved somewhere else, if that makes sense. Like when my design software freezes and you’re still clicking even though nothing is responding—sorry, that’s a weird comparison. But it’s kind of like that. I’m aware of it, but I don’t feel like I’m steering anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes, looking back on them the next day or at any other time?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I haven’t really tracked it like that the next day. Sometimes I’m just busy, like I’ll wake up and there’s already emails or I’m late getting a file out, and then I’m thinking about whether the client is going to hate the font choice, which sounds unrelated, sorry.\n\nI guess I’m not sure what I’d call it. I notice the containers or the wrappers sometimes and I don’t feel great about it, but I don’t know if that’s guilt exactly or just… “ugh, I did that.” Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? I mean, not in any intentional way like, “I ate too much so now I need to take something.” I know what laxatives are, obviously, but diuretics—those are like water pills, right?\n\nI don’t have a routine like that after. Usually after those nights I’m more just trying to clean up the containers or pretend I’m not noticing them, and then I get distracted by work stuff or my apartment being a mess, which is kind of always happening. But no, I don’t think I use anything like that after eating.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I’m… not totally sure what counts as significant. Like, work is always kind of chaotic because deadlines are chaotic, and clients send feedback at 10 p.m. like everyone is supposed to be awake and caring about kerning at that hour.

I think… maybe it starts as stress sometimes, or boredom, like if I’m sitting there with my laptop open and I’ve been staring at the same layout for too long. But once it starts, it’s not really like I’m choosing it in a normal way.

I don’t weigh myself consistently, so I don’t have, like, a chart in my head or anything.\n\nMy clothes feel different sometimes, but then I also have clothes from three different “eras” of my life, if that makes sense—like old work pants from when I actually went into an office, and then stretchy at-home clothes, and then random thrifted stuff that never fit right in the first place. So it’s hard to tell from that.\n\nI haven’t noticed anything that feels really dramatic, I guess. But I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Like, not as a thing I do after. I’ve felt nauseous sometimes if I eat too fast or too much, but I don’t—sorry, I’m trying to think if there was ever a time where I did it on purpose.\n\nI don’t really like throwing up at all. I’ll do that thing where I lie really still and scroll on my phone and tell myself I’m fine, which is probably ridiculous because then I end up watching random videos until like 2 a.m.—but no, I don’t think I make myself vomit after.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there are times when you eat a large amount of food in a short period, like during those late-night episodes, without doing anything to try to counteract the calories afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s basically what happens. Like, on late work nights especially, I’ll order what I’m calling dinner, but then it’s not just dinner. It’ll be an entrée, and then sides, and dessert, and then somehow I’m also in the kitchen getting chips or whatever snack is there.\n\nAnd it’s in this weird short window where I’m still kind of at my desk, half-working, half-watching something, and then I look at the containers and it’s like… that was a lot. I mean, at the very beginning I tell myself it’s just dinner, or just one snack, and I’m making a choice, you know? It feels like the decision already moved somewhere else, if that makes sense. I haven’t really tracked it like that the next day. Sometimes I’m just busy, like I’ll wake up and there’s already emails or I’m late getting a file out, and then I’m thinking about whether the client is going to hate the font choice, which sounds unrelated, sorry.\n\nI guess I’m not sure what I’d call it. I notice the containers or the wrappers sometimes and I don’t feel great about it, but I don’t know if that’s guilt exactly or just… “ugh, I did that.” Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? I mean, not in any intentional way like, “I ate too much so now I need to take something.” I know what laxatives are, obviously, but diuretics—those are like water pills, right?\n\nI don’t have a routine like that after. Usually after those nights I’m more just trying to clean up the containers or pretend I’m not noticing them, and then I get distracted by work stuff or my apartment being a mess, which is kind of always happening. But no, I don’t think I use anything like that after eating.\n\nDoctor: Have your eating habits interfered with your relationships, work, or other activities in a significant way?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure what counts as significant. Like, work is always kind of chaotic because deadlines are chaotic, and clients send feedback at 10 p.m. like everyone is supposed to be awake and caring about kerning at that hour.\n\nI guess I haven’t really connected the eating with relationships or activities in a clear way. I’m single, so there isn’t someone at home noticing, and with friends I can usually just… reschedule or say I’m tired, but I say that for a lot of reasons. Work-wise, I don’t know. If I’m tired the next day, I usually blame it on staying up too late on a project.\n\nSo… maybe? But I don’t have a clean answer. I haven’t noticed one big obvious consequence, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating during those late-night episodes because you can't stop yourself, or because you're trying to cope with something else, like stress or boredom?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe it starts as stress sometimes, or boredom, like if I’m sitting there with my laptop open and I’ve been staring at the same layout for too long. But once it starts, it’s not really like I’m choosing it in a normal way.\n\nThere’s this point where I’m kind of watching myself keep going back for more, or opening another package, and I’m thinking, “Okay, stop now,” but I don’t actually stop. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I can say that clearly. I mean, sometimes I’m stressed, definitely, but I’m stressed a lot, so it’s hard to separate from everything else.

Like last week I had three different versions of the same logo open, and I’d been staring at them so long they all started looking wrong, and then I was in the kitchen, and then I was eating. But I don’t know if that means I was eating because of anxiety, or if it was just late and I was tired and there was food there.

I guess I haven’t really tracked the emotion part. I usually only think about it afterward, and then I’m more annoyed with myself than anything. So… maybe, but I’m not totally sure.

It feels like way more than that. But… yeah, it’s been that kind of weekly pattern for several months now, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these late-night eating episodes by doing something to counteract the calories, like exercising more or skipping meals the next day?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know. I don’t think I’ve been tracking that in a clear way.\n\nLike, sometimes the next day is weird because if I’m rushing to get a project out, I might not eat at a normal time anyway, but that happens even when there wasn’t a late-night thing. And exercise—honestly, I’ll get these ideas like, “I should go for a walk,” but then I end up sitting at my desk adjusting kerning for two hours and forget what time it is.\n\nSo… I’m not sure if I’m doing it to make up for calories, exactly. I haven’t really noticed a pattern like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for the calories from these late-night eating episodes by restricting your food intake the next day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, the next day I might not eat much until later, but that also happens on normal workdays when I get sucked into a project and suddenly it’s 3 p.m. and I’ve only had coffee. \n\nSo I don’t know if it’s like… me deliberately trying to “make up” for it. It’s more chaotic than that, I guess. I haven’t really noticed myself making a plan like, “Okay, don’t eat today because of last night.”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your eating habits are causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m not sure how to separate the eating part from the general stress, if that makes sense. Like, I’m stressed a lot because of work, and then the food stuff kind of gets mixed into that, and then I’m annoyed at myself for spending money on delivery when I already have groceries, which reminds me I still haven’t cleaned out my fridge from last week—\n\nSorry. Um. I guess it does stress me out some, yeah, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s the eating habits by themselves or everything around it. It’s hard for me to tell.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know. I don’t weigh myself consistently, so I don’t have, like, a chart in my head or anything.\n\nMy clothes feel different sometimes, but then I also have clothes from three different “eras” of my life, if that makes sense—like old work pants from when I actually went into an office, and then stretchy at-home clothes, and then random thrifted stuff that never fit right in the first place. So it’s hard to tell from that.\n\nI haven’t noticed anything that feels really dramatic, I guess. But I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Like, not as a thing I do after. I’ve felt nauseous sometimes if I eat too fast or too much, but I don’t—sorry, I’m trying to think if there was ever a time where I did it on purpose.\n\nI don’t really like throwing up at all. I’ll do that thing where I lie really still and scroll on my phone and tell myself I’m fine, which is probably ridiculous because then I end up watching random videos until like 2 a.m.—but no, I don’t think I make myself vomit after.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there are times when you eat a large amount of food in a short period, like during those late-night episodes, without doing anything to try to counteract the calories afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s basically what happens. Like, on late work nights especially, I’ll order what I’m calling dinner, but then it’s not just dinner. It’ll be an entrée, and then sides, and dessert, and then somehow I’m also in the kitchen getting chips or whatever snack is there.\n\nAnd it’s in this weird short window where I’m still kind of at my desk, half-working, half-watching something, and then I look at the containers and it’s like… that was a lot. Definitely more than I’d eat if someone was sitting across from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not really. I mean, at the very beginning I tell myself it’s just dinner, or just one snack, and I’m making a choice, you know? But then there’s this point where I’m kind of watching myself keep going back to the app or the kitchen, like clicking back to the food, opening another package, and I’m thinking, “Okay, stop, that’s enough.”\n\nAnd then I just… don’t. It feels like the decision already moved somewhere else, if that makes sense. Like when my design software freezes and you’re still clicking even though nothing is responding—sorry, that’s a weird comparison. But it’s kind of like that. I’m aware of it, but I don’t feel like I’m steering anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes, looking back on them the next day or at any other time?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I haven’t really tracked it like that the next day. I notice the containers or the wrappers sometimes and I don’t feel great about it, but I don’t know if that’s guilt exactly or just… “ugh, I did that.” Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? I haven’t noticed one big obvious consequence, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating during those late-night episodes because you can't stop yourself, or because you're trying to cope with something else, like stress or boredom?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe it starts as stress sometimes, or boredom, like if I’m sitting there with my laptop open and I’ve been staring at the same layout for too long. But once it starts, it’s not really like I’m choosing it in a normal way.\n\nThere’s this point where I’m kind of watching myself keep going back for more, or opening another package, and I’m thinking, “Okay, stop now,” but I don’t actually stop. It feels like the decision has already slipped out of my hands, if that makes sense.\n\nSorry, I’m probably overexplaining. It’s just hard to tell where “I wanted something” ends and “I can’t stop” starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat more during those late-night episodes because you're trying to deal with feelings of stress, anxiety, or other emotions?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that clearly. I mean, sometimes I’m stressed, definitely, but I’m stressed a lot, so it’s hard to separate from everything else.\n\nLike last week I had three different versions of the same logo open, and I’d been staring at them so long they all started looking wrong, and then I was in the kitchen, and then I was eating. But I don’t know if that means I was eating because of anxiety, or if it was just late and I was tired and there was food there.\n\nI guess I haven’t really tracked the emotion part. I usually only think about it afterward, and then I’m more annoyed with myself than anything. So… maybe, but I’m not totally sure.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever fast or severely restrict your food intake after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so? I mean, not in a planned way. Like I don’t wake up and decide, “Okay, today I’m not allowed to eat,” or anything like that.

Sometimes the next morning I’m not really hungry, or I feel kind of gross and I’ll just have coffee and get distracted by work… but that also happens when I’m deep in a project, because I’ll look up and realize it’s 2 p.m. and I’ve been arguing with a font choice for four hours. So I don’t know if that counts as restricting.

I wouldn’t say I fast on purpose after it, though. At least not that I’ve noticed.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.4\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… it’s my eating. Especially on late work nights. I’m a graphic designer, so deadlines can get ridiculous, and I’ll order what I tell myself is just dinner, but then it turns into an entrée, sides, dessert… and then I’m still going into the kitchen for extra snacks.\n\nAnd it’s not really how I’d eat if someone were sitting across from me, you know? It feels like way more than that. Sorry, I’m rambling—I think that’s the main reason I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I know I don’t feel great afterward, but “guilty” feels like a specific word, you know?\n\nSometimes I’m more just sitting there thinking, why did I do that, especially if there are wrappers or containers around. But then my brain jumps to, like, the deadline, or whether the client is going to hate the mockup, or if I remembered to send the invoice—sorry. I guess I haven’t really paid attention to whether it’s guilt exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s actually the part that scares me. Like, once I start, there’s this point where I’m sort of watching myself keep going back—clicking back to the food app, or opening another package in the kitchen—and I’m thinking, okay, stop now, that’s enough.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like a normal decision anymore? It’s like the stopping part already slipped away from me. And then I’ll get distracted by work again for a second, like checking a file export or whatever, and somehow I’m eating more before I’ve really chosen to.\n\nDoctor: How often do these late-night eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Um… I didn’t realize it was that regular until I started kind of checking my delivery receipts, because I kept telling myself it was just “busy weeks.” But when I look at those and my calendar, there’s usually at least one night a week where it happens.\n\nSometimes it’s more than that, especially if a deadline overlaps with, like, client revisions or I’m staying up too late messing with layouts. But… yeah, it’s been that kind of weekly pattern for several months now, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these late-night eating episodes by doing something to counteract the calories, like exercising more or skipping meals the next day?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know. I don’t think I’ve been tracking that in a clear way.\n\nLike, sometimes the next day is weird because if I’m rushing to get a project out, I might not eat at a normal time anyway, but that happens even when there wasn’t a late-night thing. And exercise—honestly, I’ll get these ideas like, “I should go for a walk,” but then I end up sitting at my desk adjusting kerning for two hours and forget what time it is.\n\nSo… I’m not sure if I’m doing it to make up for calories, exactly. I haven’t really noticed a pattern like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for the calories from these late-night eating episodes by restricting your food intake the next day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, the next day I might not eat much until later, but that also happens on normal workdays when I get sucked into a project and suddenly it’s 3 p.m. and I’ve only had coffee. \n\nSo I don’t know if it’s like… me deliberately trying to “make up” for it. It’s more chaotic than that, I guess. I haven’t really noticed myself making a plan like, “Okay, don’t eat today because of last night.”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your eating habits are causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m not sure how to separate the eating part from the general stress, if that makes sense. Like, I’m stressed a lot because of work, and then the food stuff kind of gets mixed into that, and then I’m annoyed at myself for spending money on delivery when I already have groceries, which reminds me I still haven’t cleaned out my fridge from last week—\n\nSorry. Um. I guess it does stress me out some, yeah, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s the eating habits by themselves or everything around it. It’s hard for me to tell.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know. I don’t weigh myself consistently, so I don’t have, like, a chart in my head or anything.\n\nMy clothes feel different sometimes, but then I also have clothes from three different “eras” of my life, if that makes sense—like old work pants from when I actually went into an office, and then stretchy at-home clothes, and then random thrifted stuff that never fit right in the first place. So it’s hard to tell from that.\n\nI haven’t noticed anything that feels really dramatic, I guess. But I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Like, not as a thing I do after. I’ve felt nauseous sometimes if I eat too fast or too much, but I don’t—sorry, I’m trying to think if there was ever a time where I did it on purpose.\n\nI don’t really like throwing up at all. I’ll do that thing where I lie really still and scroll on my phone and tell myself I’m fine, which is probably ridiculous because then I end up watching random videos until like 2 a.m.—but no, I don’t think I make myself vomit after.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there are times when you eat a large amount of food in a short period, like during those late-night episodes, without doing anything to try to counteract the calories afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s basically what happens. Like, on late work nights especially, I’ll order what I’m calling dinner, but then it’s not just dinner. It’ll be an entrée, and then sides, and dessert, and then somehow I’m also in the kitchen getting chips or whatever snack is there.\n\nAnd it’s in this weird short window where I’m still kind of at my desk, half-working, half-watching something, and then I look at the containers and it’s like… that was a lot. Definitely more than I’d eat if someone was sitting across from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not really. I mean, at the very beginning I tell myself it’s just dinner, or just one snack, and I’m making a choice, you know? But then there’s this point where I’m kind of watching myself keep going back to the app or the kitchen, like clicking back to the food, opening another package, and I’m thinking, “Okay, stop, that’s enough.”\n\nAnd then I just… don’t. It feels like the decision already moved somewhere else, if that makes sense. Like when my design software freezes and you’re still clicking even though nothing is responding—sorry, that’s a weird comparison. But it’s kind of like that. I’m aware of it, but I don’t feel like I’m steering anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes, looking back on them the next day or at any other time?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I haven’t really tracked it like that the next day. Sometimes I’m just busy, like I’ll wake up and there’s already emails or I’m late getting a file out, and then I’m thinking about whether the client is going to hate the font choice, which sounds unrelated, sorry.\n\nI guess I’m not sure what I’d call it. I notice the containers or the wrappers sometimes and I don’t feel great about it, but I don’t know if that’s guilt exactly or just… “ugh, I did that.” Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? I mean, not in any intentional way like, “I ate too much so now I need to take something.” I know what laxatives are, obviously, but diuretics—those are like water pills, right?\n\nI don’t have a routine like that after. Usually after those nights I’m more just trying to clean up the containers or pretend I’m not noticing them, and then I get distracted by work stuff or my apartment being a mess, which is kind of always happening. But no, I don’t think I use anything like that after eating.\n\nDoctor: Have your eating habits interfered with your relationships, work, or other activities in a significant way?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure what counts as significant. Like, work is always kind of chaotic because deadlines are chaotic, and clients send feedback at 10 p.m. like everyone is supposed to be awake and caring about kerning at that hour.\n\nI guess I haven’t really connected the eating with relationships or activities in a clear way. I’m single, so there isn’t someone at home noticing, and with friends I can usually just… reschedule or say I’m tired, but I say that for a lot of reasons. Work-wise, I don’t know. If I’m tired the next day, I usually blame it on staying up too late on a project.\n\nSo… maybe? But I don’t have a clean answer. I haven’t noticed one big obvious consequence, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating during those late-night episodes because you can't stop yourself, or because you're trying to cope with something else, like stress or boredom?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe it starts as stress sometimes, or boredom, like if I’m sitting there with my laptop open and I’ve been staring at the same layout for too long. It feels like the decision has already slipped out of my hands, if that makes sense.\n\nSorry, I’m probably overexplaining. It’s just hard to tell where “I wanted something” ends and “I can’t stop” starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat more during those late-night episodes because you're trying to deal with feelings of stress, anxiety, or other emotions?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that clearly. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Sometimes I’m just busy, like I’ll wake up and there’s already emails or I’m late getting a file out, and then I’m thinking about whether the client is going to hate the font choice, which sounds unrelated, sorry.\n\nI guess I’m not sure what I’d call it. I notice the containers or the wrappers sometimes and I don’t feel great about it, but I don’t know if that’s guilt exactly or just… “ugh, I did that.” Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? I mean, not in any intentional way like, “I ate too much so now I need to take something.” I know what laxatives are, obviously, but diuretics—those are like water pills, right?\n\nI don’t have a routine like that after. Usually after those nights I’m more just trying to clean up the containers or pretend I’m not noticing them, and then I get distracted by work stuff or my apartment being a mess, which is kind of always happening. Yeah… I think that’s pretty much what’s been happening.

It’s usually on these late work nights, when I’m still at my desk and I tell myself I’m just ordering dinner because I forgot to eat properly or whatever. But then it’s not just dinner. It’ll be like a whole entrée, sides, dessert, and then after that I’m still going into the kitchen for chips or cookies or whatever’s there.

And it feels embarrassing to say, because if someone were sitting across from me, I would never eat that much in front of them. I’d be aware of it. But when I’m alone and it’s late, it kind of turns into… way more than I meant it to be.

And no, I don’t really do anything afterward to make up for it, not intentionally.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.4\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… it’s my eating. Especially on late work nights. I’m a graphic designer, so deadlines can get ridiculous, and I’ll order what I tell myself is just dinner, but then it turns into an entrée, sides, dessert… and then I’m still going into the kitchen for extra snacks.\n\nAnd it’s not really how I’d eat if someone were sitting across from me, you know? It feels like way more than that. Sorry, I’m rambling—I think that’s the main reason I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I know I don’t feel great afterward, but “guilty” feels like a specific word, you know?\n\nSometimes I’m more just sitting there thinking, why did I do that, especially if there are wrappers or containers around. But then my brain jumps to, like, the deadline, or whether the client is going to hate the mockup, or if I remembered to send the invoice—sorry. I guess I haven’t really paid attention to whether it’s guilt exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s actually the part that scares me. Like, once I start, there’s this point where I’m sort of watching myself keep going back—clicking back to the food app, or opening another package in the kitchen—and I’m thinking, okay, stop now, that’s enough.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like a normal decision anymore? It’s like the stopping part already slipped away from me. And then I’ll get distracted by work again for a second, like checking a file export or whatever, and somehow I’m eating more before I’ve really chosen to.\n\nDoctor: How often do these late-night eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Um… I didn’t realize it was that regular until I started kind of checking my delivery receipts, because I kept telling myself it was just “busy weeks.” But when I look at those and my calendar, there’s usually at least one night a week where it happens.\n\nSometimes it’s more than that, especially if a deadline overlaps with, like, client revisions or I’m staying up too late messing with layouts. But… yeah, it’s been that kind of weekly pattern for several months now, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these late-night eating episodes by doing something to counteract the calories, like exercising more or skipping meals the next day?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know. I don’t think I’ve been tracking that in a clear way.\n\nLike, sometimes the next day is weird because if I’m rushing to get a project out, I might not eat at a normal time anyway, but that happens even when there wasn’t a late-night thing. And exercise—honestly, I’ll get these ideas like, “I should go for a walk,” but then I end up sitting at my desk adjusting kerning for two hours and forget what time it is.\n\nSo… I’m not sure if I’m doing it to make up for calories, exactly. I haven’t really noticed a pattern like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for the calories from these late-night eating episodes by restricting your food intake the next day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, the next day I might not eat much until later, but that also happens on normal workdays when I get sucked into a project and suddenly it’s 3 p.m. and I’ve only had coffee. \n\nSo I don’t know if it’s like… me deliberately trying to “make up” for it. It’s more chaotic than that, I guess. I haven’t really noticed myself making a plan like, “Okay, don’t eat today because of last night.”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your eating habits are causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m not sure how to separate the eating part from the general stress, if that makes sense. Like, I’m stressed a lot because of work, and then the food stuff kind of gets mixed into that, and then I’m annoyed at myself for spending money on delivery when I already have groceries, which reminds me I still haven’t cleaned out my fridge from last week—\n\nSorry. Um. I guess it does stress me out some, yeah, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s the eating habits by themselves or everything around it. It’s hard for me to tell.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know. I don’t weigh myself consistently, so I don’t have, like, a chart in my head or anything.\n\nMy clothes feel different sometimes, but then I also have clothes from three different “eras” of my life, if that makes sense—like old work pants from when I actually went into an office, and then stretchy at-home clothes, and then random thrifted stuff that never fit right in the first place. So it’s hard to tell from that.\n\nI haven’t noticed anything that feels really dramatic, I guess. But I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Like, not as a thing I do after. I’ve felt nauseous sometimes if I eat too fast or too much, but I don’t—sorry, I’m trying to think if there was ever a time where I did it on purpose.\n\nI don’t really like throwing up at all. I’ll do that thing where I lie really still and scroll on my phone and tell myself I’m fine, which is probably ridiculous because then I end up watching random videos until like 2 a.m.—but no, I don’t think I make myself vomit after.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there are times when you eat a large amount of food in a short period, like during those late-night episodes, without doing anything to try to counteract the calories afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s basically what happens. Like, on late work nights especially, I’ll order what I’m calling dinner, but then it’s not just dinner. It’ll be an entrée, and then sides, and dessert, and then somehow I’m also in the kitchen getting chips or whatever snack is there.\n\nAnd it’s in this weird short window where I’m still kind of at my desk, half-working, half-watching something, and then I look at the containers and it’s like… that was a lot. Definitely more than I’d eat if someone was sitting across from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not really. I mean, at the very beginning I tell myself it’s just dinner, or just one snack, and I’m making a choice, you know? But then there’s this point where I’m kind of watching myself keep going back to the app or the kitchen, like clicking back to the food, opening another package, and I’m thinking, “Okay, stop, that’s enough.”\n\nAnd then I just… don’t. It feels like the decision already moved somewhere else, if that makes sense. Like when my design software freezes and you’re still clicking even though nothing is responding—sorry, that’s a weird comparison. But it’s kind of like that. I’m aware of it, but I don’t feel like I’m steering anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes, looking back on them the next day or at any other time?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I haven’t really tracked it like that the next day. Sometimes I’m just busy, like I’ll wake up and there’s already emails or I’m late getting a file out, and then I’m thinking about whether the client is going to hate the font choice, which sounds unrelated, sorry.\n\nI guess I’m not sure what I’d call it. I notice the containers or the wrappers sometimes and I don’t feel great about it, but I don’t know if that’s guilt exactly or just… “ugh, I did that.” Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? I mean, not in any intentional way like, “I ate too much so now I need to take something.” I know what laxatives are, obviously, but diuretics—those are like water pills, right?\n\nI don’t have a routine like that after. Usually after those nights I’m more just trying to clean up the containers or pretend I’m not noticing them, and then I get distracted by work stuff or my apartment being a mess, which is kind of always happening. But no, I don’t think I use anything like that after eating.\n\nDoctor: Have your eating habits interfered with your relationships, work, or other activities in a significant way?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure what counts as significant. Like, work is always kind of chaotic because deadlines are chaotic, and clients send feedback at 10 p.m. like everyone is supposed to be awake and caring about kerning at that hour.\n\nI guess I haven’t really connected the eating with relationships or activities in a clear way. I’m single, so there isn’t someone at home noticing, and with friends I can usually just… reschedule or say I’m tired, but I say that for a lot of reasons. Work-wise, I don’t know. If I’m tired the next day, I usually blame it on staying up too late on a project.\n\nSo… maybe? But I don’t have a clean answer. I haven’t noticed one big obvious consequence, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating during those late-night episodes because you can't stop yourself, or because you're trying to cope with something else, like stress or boredom?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe it starts as stress sometimes, or boredom, like if I’m sitting there with my laptop open and I’ve been staring at the same layout for too long. But once it starts, it’s not really like I’m choosing it in a normal way.\n\nThere’s this point where I’m kind of watching myself keep going back for more, or opening another package, and I’m thinking, “Okay, stop now,” but I don’t actually stop. It feels like the decision has already slipped out of my hands, if that makes sense.\n\nSorry, I’m probably overexplaining. It’s just hard to tell where “I wanted something” ends and “I can’t stop” starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat more during those late-night episodes because you're trying to deal with feelings of stress, anxiety, or other emotions?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that clearly. I mean, sometimes I’m stressed, definitely, but I’m stressed a lot, so it’s hard to separate from everything else.\n\nLike last week I had three different versions of the same logo open, and I’d been staring at them so long they all started looking wrong, and then I was in the kitchen, and then I was eating. But I don’t know if that means I was eating because of anxiety, or if it was just late and I was tired and there was food there.\n\nI guess I haven’t really tracked the emotion part. I usually only think about it afterward, and then I’m more annoyed with myself than anything. So… maybe, but I’m not totally sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever fast or severely restrict your food intake after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, not in a planned way. Like I don’t wake up and decide, “Okay, today I’m not allowed to eat,” or anything like that.\n\nSometimes the next morning I’m not really hungry, or I feel kind of gross and I’ll just have coffee and get distracted by work… but that also happens when I’m deep in a project, because I’ll look up and realize it’s 2 p.m. and I’ve been arguing with a font choice for four hours. So I don’t know if that counts as restricting.\n\nI wouldn’t say I fast on purpose after it, though. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short time without trying to make up for it afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s pretty much what’s been happening.\n\nIt’s usually on these late work nights, when I’m still at my desk and I tell myself I’m just ordering dinner because I forgot to eat properly or whatever. But then it’s not just dinner. It’ll be like a whole entrée, sides, dessert, and then after that I’m still going into the kitchen for chips or cookies or whatever’s there.\n\nAnd it feels embarrassing to say, because if someone were sitting across from me, I would never eat that much in front of them. I’d be aware of it. But when I’m alone and it’s late, it kind of turns into… way more than I meant it to be.\n\nAnd no, I don’t really do anything afterward to make up for it, not intentionally.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to purge after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know. I mean, I don’t think of myself as doing that. When you say purge, you mean like making myself throw up or taking something, right?\n\nI haven’t noticed myself doing anything like that after. Usually I just feel uncomfortable and kind of avoid thinking about it, or I’ll clean up the wrappers because I hate seeing them in the morning. Which is funny, not funny, but my kitchen trash is right by this cabinet door that never closes properly, and every time I’m standing there I think, “I really need to fix that,” and then I never do.\n\nBut no… not that I can think of. I don’t try to make myself sick afterward.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Yeah… that’s the part that bothers me, honestly. It’s not like I’m making some clear choice the whole time.

I’m a graphic designer, so deadlines can get ridiculous, and I’ll order what I tell myself is just dinner, but then it turns into an entrée, sides, dessert… and then I’m still going into the kitchen for extra snacks.\n\nAnd it’s not really how I’d eat if someone were sitting across from me, you know? It feels like way more than that. Sorry, I’m rambling—I think that’s the main reason I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I know I don’t feel great afterward, but “guilty” feels like a specific word, you know?\n\nSometimes I’m more just sitting there thinking, why did I do that, especially if there are wrappers or containers around. But then my brain jumps to, like, the deadline, or whether the client is going to hate the mockup, or if I remembered to send the invoice—sorry. I guess I haven’t really paid attention to whether it’s guilt exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s actually the part that scares me. Like, once I start, there’s this point where I’m sort of watching myself keep going back—clicking back to the food app, or opening another package in the kitchen—and I’m thinking, okay, stop now, that’s enough.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like a normal decision anymore? It’s like the stopping part already slipped away from me. And then I’ll get distracted by work again for a second, like checking a file export or whatever, and somehow I’m eating more before I’ve really chosen to.\n\nDoctor: How often do these late-night eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Um… I didn’t realize it was that regular until I started kind of checking my delivery receipts, because I kept telling myself it was just “busy weeks.” But when I look at those and my calendar, there’s usually at least one night a week where it happens.\n\nSometimes it’s more than that, especially if a deadline overlaps with, like, client revisions or I’m staying up too late messing with layouts. But… yeah, it’s been that kind of weekly pattern for several months now, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these late-night eating episodes by doing something to counteract the calories, like exercising more or skipping meals the next day?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know. I don’t think I’ve been tracking that in a clear way.\n\nLike, sometimes the next day is weird because if I’m rushing to get a project out, I might not eat at a normal time anyway, but that happens even when there wasn’t a late-night thing. And exercise—honestly, I’ll get these ideas like, “I should go for a walk,” but then I end up sitting at my desk adjusting kerning for two hours and forget what time it is.\n\nSo… I’m not sure if I’m doing it to make up for calories, exactly. I haven’t really noticed a pattern like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for the calories from these late-night eating episodes by restricting your food intake the next day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, the next day I might not eat much until later, but that also happens on normal workdays when I get sucked into a project and suddenly it’s 3 p.m. and I’ve only had coffee. \n\nSo I don’t know if it’s like… me deliberately trying to “make up” for it. It’s more chaotic than that, I guess. I haven’t really noticed myself making a plan like, “Okay, don’t eat today because of last night.”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your eating habits are causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m not sure how to separate the eating part from the general stress, if that makes sense. Like, I’m stressed a lot because of work, and then the food stuff kind of gets mixed into that, and then I’m annoyed at myself for spending money on delivery when I already have groceries, which reminds me I still haven’t cleaned out my fridge from last week—\n\nSorry. Um. I guess it does stress me out some, yeah, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s the eating habits by themselves or everything around it. It’s hard for me to tell.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know. I don’t weigh myself consistently, so I don’t have, like, a chart in my head or anything.\n\nMy clothes feel different sometimes, but then I also have clothes from three different “eras” of my life, if that makes sense—like old work pants from when I actually went into an office, and then stretchy at-home clothes, and then random thrifted stuff that never fit right in the first place. So it’s hard to tell from that.\n\nI haven’t noticed anything that feels really dramatic, I guess. But I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Like, not as a thing I do after. I’ve felt nauseous sometimes if I eat too fast or too much, but I don’t—sorry, I’m trying to think if there was ever a time where I did it on purpose.\n\nI don’t really like throwing up at all. I’ll do that thing where I lie really still and scroll on my phone and tell myself I’m fine, which is probably ridiculous because then I end up watching random videos until like 2 a.m.—but no, I don’t think I make myself vomit after.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there are times when you eat a large amount of food in a short period, like during those late-night episodes, without doing anything to try to counteract the calories afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s basically what happens. Like, on late work nights especially, I’ll order what I’m calling dinner, but then it’s not just dinner. It’ll be an entrée, and then sides, and dessert, and then somehow I’m also in the kitchen getting chips or whatever snack is there.\n\nAnd it’s in this weird short window where I’m still kind of at my desk, half-working, half-watching something, and then I look at the containers and it’s like… that was a lot. Definitely more than I’d eat if someone was sitting across from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not really. I mean, at the very beginning I tell myself it’s just dinner, or just one snack, and I’m making a choice, you know? But then there’s this point where I’m kind of watching myself keep going back to the app or the kitchen, like clicking back to the food, opening another package, and I’m thinking, “Okay, stop, that’s enough.”\n\nAnd then I just… don’t. It feels like the decision already moved somewhere else, if that makes sense. Like when my design software freezes and you’re still clicking even though nothing is responding—sorry, that’s a weird comparison. But it’s kind of like that. I’m aware of it, but I don’t feel like I’m steering anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes, looking back on them the next day or at any other time?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I haven’t really tracked it like that the next day. Sometimes I’m just busy, like I’ll wake up and there’s already emails or I’m late getting a file out, and then I’m thinking about whether the client is going to hate the font choice, which sounds unrelated, sorry.\n\nI guess I’m not sure what I’d call it. I notice the containers or the wrappers sometimes and I don’t feel great about it, but I don’t know if that’s guilt exactly or just… “ugh, I did that.” Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? I mean, not in any intentional way like, “I ate too much so now I need to take something.” I know what laxatives are, obviously, but diuretics—those are like water pills, right?\n\nI don’t have a routine like that after. Usually after those nights I’m more just trying to clean up the containers or pretend I’m not noticing them, and then I get distracted by work stuff or my apartment being a mess, which is kind of always happening. But no, I don’t think I use anything like that after eating.\n\nDoctor: Have your eating habits interfered with your relationships, work, or other activities in a significant way?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure what counts as significant. Like, work is always kind of chaotic because deadlines are chaotic, and clients send feedback at 10 p.m. like everyone is supposed to be awake and caring about kerning at that hour.\n\nI guess I haven’t really connected the eating with relationships or activities in a clear way. I’m single, so there isn’t someone at home noticing, and with friends I can usually just… reschedule or say I’m tired, but I say that for a lot of reasons. Work-wise, I don’t know. If I’m tired the next day, I usually blame it on staying up too late on a project.\n\nSo… maybe? But I don’t have a clean answer. I haven’t noticed one big obvious consequence, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating during those late-night episodes because you can't stop yourself, or because you're trying to cope with something else, like stress or boredom?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe it starts as stress sometimes, or boredom, like if I’m sitting there with my laptop open and I’ve been staring at the same layout for too long. But once it starts, it’s not really like I’m choosing it in a normal way.\n\nThere’s this point where I’m kind of watching myself keep going back for more, or opening another package, and I’m thinking, “Okay, stop now,” but I don’t actually stop. It feels like the decision has already slipped out of my hands, if that makes sense.\n\nSorry, I’m probably overexplaining. It’s just hard to tell where “I wanted something” ends and “I can’t stop” starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat more during those late-night episodes because you're trying to deal with feelings of stress, anxiety, or other emotions?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that clearly. I mean, sometimes I’m stressed, definitely, but I’m stressed a lot, so it’s hard to separate from everything else.\n\nLike last week I had three different versions of the same logo open, and I’d been staring at them so long they all started looking wrong, and then I was in the kitchen, and then I was eating. But I don’t know if that means I was eating because of anxiety, or if it was just late and I was tired and there was food there.\n\nI guess I haven’t really tracked the emotion part. I usually only think about it afterward, and then I’m more annoyed with myself than anything. So… maybe, but I’m not totally sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever fast or severely restrict your food intake after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, not in a planned way. Like I don’t wake up and decide, “Okay, today I’m not allowed to eat,” or anything like that.\n\nSometimes the next morning I’m not really hungry, or I feel kind of gross and I’ll just have coffee and get distracted by work… but that also happens when I’m deep in a project, because I’ll look up and realize it’s 2 p.m. and I’ve been arguing with a font choice for four hours. So I don’t know if that counts as restricting.\n\nI wouldn’t say I fast on purpose after it, though. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short time without trying to make up for it afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s pretty much what’s been happening.\n\nIt’s usually on these late work nights, when I’m still at my desk and I tell myself I’m just ordering dinner because I forgot to eat properly or whatever. But then it’s not just dinner. It’ll be like a whole entrée, sides, dessert, and then after that I’m still going into the kitchen for chips or cookies or whatever’s there.\n\nAnd it feels embarrassing to say, because if someone were sitting across from me, I would never eat that much in front of them. I’d be aware of it. But when I’m alone and it’s late, it kind of turns into… way more than I meant it to be.\n\nAnd no, I don’t really do anything afterward to make up for it, not intentionally.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to purge after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know. I mean, I don’t think of myself as doing that. When you say purge, you mean like making myself throw up or taking something, right?\n\nI haven’t noticed myself doing anything like that after. Usually I just feel uncomfortable and kind of avoid thinking about it, or I’ll clean up the wrappers because I hate seeing them in the morning. Which is funny, not funny, but my kitchen trash is right by this cabinet door that never closes properly, and every time I’m standing there I think, “I really need to fix that,” and then I never do.\n\nBut no… not that I can think of. I don’t try to make myself sick afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s the part that bothers me, honestly. It’s not like I’m making some clear choice the whole time.\n\nI’ll start with one thing, and then there’s this point where I’m kind of watching myself keep going — like clicking back to the food app or opening another package — and I’m thinking, “Okay, stop, you don’t need more,” but it doesn’t really feel like I’m the one deciding anymore. Or like the decision already happened and I’m just… following it.\n\nSorry, that sounds dramatic. It’s just weird, because with work I can be so controlling about tiny things, like moving a logo two pixels for half an hour, but with this it’s like my hands are doing it before I’ve really agreed to it."
